.i To Advertisers.
We devote all the space we can possibly spare
to advertisers in tbls moraine's paper, and
those who find that their advertisements are left
oat will pardon it to the necessity of giving a
i»»r portion of our columns to the reading pub
h'e. The demand for intelligence is not only in
satiable, but It is absolutely necessary to the
welfare of the people and the city that snueh of
that which we publish should no to the pnblie.
We divide oar space between the reader and the
advertiser, and that is the best we can do for
both clanes. The advertiser will understand
that the earlier in the dsy he tires ns what he
wishes to have published, and the smaller the
rpsee into which be condenses it. the more
likely it is to find a place incur columns. ..t.
To Our Conutrr Subscribers.
Ws have received many letters of inquiry from
oar country subscribers desiring information as
to the probable time wben we shall* be able to
supply them with The Tbibukc, us of old. We
reply at the very earliest possible moment. The
principal difficulty in the way now is the Chicago
Post Office, which is unable to distribute onr
paper for (he mails, owing to the disarrangement
of their system uf doing that work. Another
difficulty, which will causa some dolay, is the
destruction of the machinery used in our office
for putting the names of the subscribers upon
the potters. These are the leading dif-
Acuities in the way; but wo hope this will be
remedied within a very few days, when we shall
'-he in shape to resume our relations with our
country subscribers. In thetaeantlmo, although
the capacity of our power press is limited, we
hopo each day to send a few thousand copies of
The Tribune into the country by train boys- We
beg the indulgence ot our country friends, as
suring them that no effort within human power
will be spared to bring perfect order ont of the
present chaos.
4ST*We have full and perfect copies ofour sub*
sedption lists.
THE TRIBUNE BUILDING.
4 server of The Tribune
building made by Burling, the archi
tect, shows that few of the "walls or floors
will require to be taken down in order to
render.lt habitable again. It is believed
that an expense of fifty thousand dollars
will rebuild the damaged portions. Wc
shall commence rebuilding as soon as the
_ plans and specifications can be drawn.
UNDER JURY-MASTS.
We must stilt ask the forbearance of
our friends under circumstances which
we can net altogether control and which
they do not altogether understand. The
demand for The Tribune is from 50,000 to
60,000 copies a day and we can not pos
ibly supply one-third of that number.
The paper has been printed for the last
week with such type as we could sud
denly lay our bands on, aided by a tamely
supply from our friends in Cincinnati.
and the press-work has had to be
done on two inefficient one-cylinder
presses. instead of the' two
eight-cylinder presses which were at our
command before the fire. In our thus
poorly appointed and extemporized office,
and with these two small presses, the paper
has been daily published under extreme
difficulties. Nor has'this been our only
trouble... Our neighbor of the Evening
Journal was as destitute as ourselves, and
such conveniences and appliances as we
could command wc cheerfully, of course,
gave them the use of With that
paper webavo taken turn and turn about,
night and day, wsUi our scanty type and
small presses, thus, in fact,
issuing two daily journals with materials
altogether inefficient for the demands of
our own. Under such circumstances wc
shall not appeal in vain to the good-fccl
ing and forbearance of our friends, both
readers and advertisers. Our new type is
already received and will soon be in place.
Preparations arc rapidly making for put
ting up a new four-cylinder press which
in a few days will be in position, and then
wc shall be in condition to meet all the
demands upon its. Wc crave patience
while we sail under jury-masts.
UNION PASSENGER DEPOT,
the sale ortTic'frrountf' on tnc norm efid'oi
l.ake Park to the railway companies for
tho erection of a great passenger station
for all tbe railways entering tbe city, a
project has been started for erecting sucb
a depot in tbe Vest Division.
A depot of tbe character proposed would
cost not less than three millions of dollars.
As the city grows and extends outward
beyond its present limits, tbe difficulty
and delay in reaching a depot on a level
with the streets, and crossing a street
every three hundred feet, will increase,
and the consequent interruption to traffic
on the streets will, in time, become exas
perating to tbe public. The history of
such city depots in New York has been
that every few years tbe railways
have been compelled to move
away from their down town sta
tions and go farther out. Ten
years ago the depot of the Hudson River
i Railway was at Chambers street. Now it
is at Thirtieth street, and in a short time it
will be still farther away. Ten years ago
the depot of the New Haven Railway
was at Canal street, and that of the Har
lem Railway at White street Now they
arc at Twenty-seventh street, and arc pre
-1 paring to take another flight ‘But to
abandon a three million-dollardopot; which:
cannot be used for any other purpose,
is a different thing from selling out
property which is available for other
uses. If Chicago shall spread out later
ally as fast in the next ten years as in the
past ten, it will take the trains on the Fort
Wayne Road two hours lo nm from En
-1 to their present station. In order
ring all4bcrailways to a common cen
tre at any other place than oh the lake
bore, a perfect wilderness of tracks will
need to. be provided, and these will be a
constant and increasing jeopardy to life
and limb. If each street is “viaducted”
the property-owners must be com
pensated for damages, as provided
by -the new constitution, such
damage to .be ascertained by a
iury. If the railways attempt to depress
Jicir tracks below the sevrers, their road
■_: 3 will fill with water and require to be
ept dry by pumping.
On the lake shore none of these difficuh
b are to be encountered. Trains can
3e run at the highest rate of speed with
out jeopardizing life or properly, and the
uture growth cannot add future'ohslroc-
to tbennslncss of the roads. The ad-
antages of that situation are so marked
.hat there is* no comparison possible hc
ween it and any other locality in or
iround thd clty.

One house of the Legislature baa passed
bill to relieve the canal of the lieu upon
resources by refunding to the city of
—-Jhicago two and one-half millions'of dol
rs expended on it, with interest, amount
ng in all to $2,955,340. This sum.is to be
*_ed by tbc'saic of State C per cent,
J payable after ten years. The bonds
be delivered to Chicago, November
ae-fifth, nor more than one-third of said
f ondß, shall be applied-to the reconstruct
on of bridges, and of public buildings do
;*d by;fire, upon the original sites,
ad the.remainder shall be applied Hlo the
.Tentbf the Interest, on the bonded
:bti. and' tho mwotenance of the'polic'd
id fire departments.
The bill will probably pass the State Son-
withont change. It appears that the
egislatnre agrees with the Governor that
e spiritvof the CrartUntioo rembrace
I
such a calamity as tbat which bas befallen
ibis city, in the special causes warranting
the Slate in an increase of the debt be
yond $250,000. The bill* leaves iho debt
standing against the city—principal and
interest —but it gives us the money for
present uses, when we have no other
means of raising it
WHAT IS SPARED US.
Our columns have been so extensively
occupied daring the past week with re
ports of the enormous losses of life and
property In the late fire, that there is some
danger that the damage sustained will be
over-estimated. True, we have seen 2,500
acres in the most central portion of the
city swept bare, 20,000 buildings destroyed,
and 100,000 persons rendered homeless,
the total pecuniary loss being not less
than $300,000,000; but we have still a
great deal left. We may roughly estimate
the situation as follows:
Above 50,000 persons have left the city;
population remaining 260,000.
Five grain elevators wore burned, with
1,600,000 bushels of grain; leaving us
with eleven grain warehouses intact, con
taining 5,000,000 bushels.
One half of our stocks of pork products
were burned up, with the same proportion
of flour.
Of lumber, 50,000,000 feet were burned;
the stock remaining is 240,000,000 feet
Of coal 80,000 tons were burned up; we
Have 79,000 tons on hand.
Our stock of leather was decreased one
quarter, the valuo.of that burned np being
$95,000.
The greater portion of tbo stocks of gro
ceries, dry goods, and boots and shoes
were burned up, with more than one half
the ready-made clothing; but the quanti
ties destroyed were scarcely equal to more
than a three weeks 1 supply, and arc now
being rapidly replaced.
Not more than 10 per cent of tbo cur
rency was destroyed, by the fire; we have
30,000 houses left standing, and our real
estate could not burn up.
A careful average of these larger items*
with smaller ones that need not be cun
tnerated,-shows that the city of Chicago
has suffered a loss of not less than 20,
nor more than 25 per cent, on her
total assets—real and personal. Thcloss
is a great one; but, so.far from irretrieva
ble, that we may confidently hope to see a
return to former prosperity ere long. The
ratio of increase during the past thirty-four
I years has averaged 10 1-2 per cent per an
num. This rale would restore the status
of a mouth ago, within three years. Making
every due allowance for*the terrible set
back experienced, there can be no doubt
that five years hence, at most, the exhibit
of population, wealth, commerce, and
manufactures will be greater than a mouth
ago. Already the city is rising from its
ashes at a rapid rate, thanks to the large
hearted liberality of our countrymen
everywhere, and the resistless energy of
our. own citizens. Let the faint-hearted
ones take courage; the real workers have
not wasted a moment in regret; after they
have repaired their losses there will be no
occasion for it
TEMPORARY SHELTER
W. S. Johnston, Esq., of Luke -Forest,
writes to Mr. FarwcU stating that he owns
200 lota on Milwaukee avenue and vicin
ity, and ho offers the free rent of these for
three years for tbe purpose of erecting on
them cheap houses for the homeless. The
committee of the Aid Society having in
charge the matter of relief arc industri
ously at work, seeking out all those who
have been rendered homeless by the fire,
and, according to the circumstances of
each cose, furnishing them with lumber,
nails, and the other material necessary to
tbe construcliou of cheap bat comfortable
habitations. There is, of course, a nat
ural desire on the part of many
to have these buildings upon the sites
of the old homes, and, whenever the needy
sufferer is. the owner of the lot, this desire
most be gratified. In all cases tbe proper
ly in building is retained. The cost of the
materials is really a loan to the sufferer.
A proper regard for public. safety requires
some system in tbo location of these build-
UUih Hst °'sGUfro c co2iifagra\mn fl. T '°st
should never bo admitted that these struc
tures are to become permanent The oc
cupants should be encouraged to look for
ward in the spring, or as soon thereafter,
as possible, to finding some other bouse
provided with all the sanitary comforts and
conveniences, and to leave these temporary
structures as soon as their wages
will authorize them to do so. All
thought of pauperism ought to bo dis
couraged. Every mutt to whom one of
these dwellings shall be given should be
enrolled as a laborer, mechanic, or work
man in some form, and made self-sup
porting. To widows there will necessarily
have, in addition to a building, to be
given supplies daring the winter, but even
in such cases there ought, to be cmplov
ment given in some form. Suffering and
want must bo prevented shelter must bo
furnished to ever}- homeless i person but
there must be no mendicancy in any form,
save where from sickness or other disabil
ity it may bo unavoidable. The offer of
Colonel Johnston is a liberal one and
ought to be accepted; other gentlemen
owning unoccupied land might make
similar offers, that the committee may
erect the buildings as rapidly as they are
Applied for.
ATROCIOUS FABRICATIONS
The papers of the Eastern cities are
publishing personal experiences of persons
professing to have been sufferers by the
fire in this city. These stories are for tbie
most part fabrications. The,descriptions
given of murders and rapes by bullies,
thieves, and ruffians,during the progress of
the tire, arc wretched inventions. A re
spectable Kew York paper gives room to
a long recital of the narrow escape of 200
abandoned.women from their lodgings in
Reynolds Block. The pictures of ob
scenity and profanity arc drawn by an ex
pert, but arc wholly destitute of truth. -No
women of that, or any other class, occu
pied-rooms in that block/ The fire was
horrible enough in'all its details;: there
»was a full aharprof.Tbc selfishness' and
bcurtlcssuoss peculiar to human nature;
there were frequent cases of drunk
enness and indifference to the im.
pending calamity, but there was
no general brutality, fiendishness,
nor crime. f l be thousand stories of in
cendiarism and crime during the progress
of the fire are gross exaggerations, per
versions or pure
was liuks time for:stealing,; the firefwas
too close upon the heels of holiest men
and thieves, and but little even of what
was stolon could be carried off.
Tbo news of the fire brought hither
thousands of thieves from other cities bul
rhesc did not reach hero uutil Wednesday
and Thursday, and by -that time public
vigilance had been arroosed, and* their
stay was short and unprofitable. The
suffering of our people was terrible
enough; it needs ,no exaggeration to
make it the most extensive scene of hu
man misery; but for the credit of common
humanity :we desire to stamp these ato
nes of murder and other no less atrocious
crimes as false in every particular.
BACK OTKBBB&
We hare received numerous letters, and
telegrams asking for complete filcs~dr
Tbk Tribuke from the date.*of the fire.
We arc unable to supply even so
much os a single number. Every copy
which we have been able to print has bceu
snatched up by eager purchaser! the men
ment it has come-from the Scss-.: We.
could have sold four times as many if we
could have printed them, and of the first
number, containing the report of the fire,
ten times as many as we could print The
best we can do toward furnishing the out
side world with any farther information
than It has already obtained fs to advise
people in the city who have saved copies
to send them to their friends abroad, un
less they hold them too precious to part
with them at all. At all events, no copies
should be wasted or destroyed, for they
furnish, in their advertisements as well as
reading matter, the most complete and
graphic history that cap possibly be made,
of the direful period through which we
we have been passing We understand
that the Post Office is now forwarding
newspapers, after the week’s interruption
of that traffic. 'Everybody who owns a
Tiubcke of the pastor present week ought
carefully to lay it by or send it to bis
friends. Wo shall bo glad to gel back all
the copies obtainable at our counters.
Letter from lion. C. Lamed.
At the time of the fire In this city Uoa. C.
Lamed, the well-known lawyer, was In Provi
dence, R, I. As there was a hearty disposition
to contribute fnnds for onr relief, but some
doubts as to the proper persons to whom such
relief should be sent, Mr. Lamed sent the fol
lowing letter to the Providence Journo?, which
bad the efieetto bring ont donations in the most
liberal manner:
Pbovidsnck, Oct. 14.1872.
To tie Editor of tie Daily Journal:
A« there I? naturally a great solicitude felt by
the public respecting the lately and proper ap
plication of the large amount* wbleb are being
<o generously contributed to the* relief of the
suffering people of Chicago, it will, doubtless,
afford great satisfaction to those contributing to
know that the persons l-> and through -whom
these funds are to be transmitted and distributed
are men of character and integrity. I have been
a citizen of Chicago for more than twenty years,
and bare been for many yean well acquainted
with Colonel R. B. Mason. Mayor. David A.
Qage, Cily Treasurer. aod'C. O. P. Jlvlden,'
Treasurer of tbo Relief Committee, atad know
them to bo gentlomeo of high character and un
sullied intetrUy. Every dollar .which iacn-,
trusted to either of them will' be os .safe as it
would be in tbo bands of any of your most trust
worthy citizens, and will be carefully and faith
folly applied to throbject for which it is donat
ed. lam about to leave for Chicago, and shall
take pleasure unou my arrivingtbere In inform
ing yourTlelicf Committee'more folly in reg&yd
to the arrangements whleb have been made, and
as to any special needs which shall require to
Ibe met. Very respectfully, B. 0. Lscsed.

To the Editor of The Chicago Tribune
Owing to the very large numnerof casesof
diarrhoea and gastric irritation, both in -adults
and children, now occurring from the drinking
<>l the water supplied to the inhabitants. 1 feci
that I ooght to warn people against using such
without Previously boiling or JtUeringU.

The .yitein of relief Inaugurated on Tuesday

lor,

retrenchment.
Tho following communication from tho Mayor
was read:
Gentlemen: I have made to all tho heads of
tho departments ollhe city, government a com
munication, a copy of which is hereto attaeaeu.
I respeettully request and require youroody to
uuthoriio and require your Finance tKimmittoc
i«» make a rigid examination of the different de
partments of the city governments to the end
I hat no department may be allowed
lo make unnecessary expenses at this time,
jmd to son that the most rigid economy is.
maintained in all matters, connected with the'
city government. It is known that nearly one
half of the taxable portion of the property of
I he city has been destroyed, and it is well known
lo you, tbata very largo part of the real prop
erty has been burnt over, so that it-is unablo to
respond to tho luxes ot tho present year called
for by the annual appropriation bill passed io
June last. In fact cue entire people bavnsuffor
ed severely by the great calamity that has visited
□s. and virtually arc unable to meet- the tax ol
fiiteen mills upon the dollar which itwas expect
ed would bu levied. Such a lax at this time
would not only ho very oppressive to the people,
but I believe, would bo ruiuous to the
interests of tho city. The necessity for
many of tho improvements and
disbursements contemplated by tho annual ap
propriation bill u" longer, exists, and
tho present tax lory should bo reduced accord
ingly. From a rough estimate made as to the
appropriations which are now lo be dispensed
with, 1 am of tbo opinion tbatthe tax levy
fur the present yoar need not exceed seven mills
on the dollar. I will rtspccttnlly request that
you ask your Finance Committee tu confer with
tho beads ot departments and the financial and
law officers of the City, and to report a tax order
by which the taxes will be reduced to tho lowest
possible amount. I have no doubt hot that
your honorable body realizes the necessity ot
retrenchment snd tcc greatest possible economy
;n ail our city affairs at this crisfs r *and- will
cheerfully do alf to accomplish it."

SEW HUE MVtTW.
The Board of Public Works presented the fol
lowing communication and ordinance, which
was referred to the Committee- on Firo and
Water, to report nn Mond iy.
Gkstlkuen:*Tlio recent fire that has devas
tated the city has aroused among our merchant*
and property owners a deep and -earnest feeling
concerning the insecurity of life and property.
on account of tho contracted fire limits and the
almost universal practice of erecting wooden'
buildlogi.
Wo are informed by business men that there
is plenty of credit to bj bad. ami capital to b
obtained for rebuilding the burnt district, pro
vided capitalists and those seeking investments
can be a-sured that these investments wilt be
made secure.
The board nro satisfied that an extension of
the present firo limits in the South Division,
and a rigid adherence to the ordinance would do
much toward insuring tbs security of tifo and
property, ami that it would contribute in a great
measure to restore the confidence in Chicago in
vestments, which has been so shaken' by tho re
cent disaster which has befallen oarcity. We,
therefore, recommend the passage’of the accom
panying ordinance.”-
